The product is BUSL-1.1 (source-available): the base grant is non-production use only; the **Additional Use Grant** extends limited production use ("Personal use and individual self-hosting are permitted; commercial hosting or resale of the Licensed Work is not") — so individuals already use it in production for free. It converts to MIT at the earlier of the Change Date (2029-06-10 / v1.0 GA + 2 years) **and** the BUSL per-version 4-year cap. This ADR supersedes the institutional-market and undecided-revenue framing. *(The grant and Change Date quoted here are the 2026-06 wording. `LICENSE` was rewritten on 2026-10-09; see the 2026-10-09 amendments to §3, §5 and §6.)*

**Decision §1 — Audience: individuals.** Target = individuals (writers, editors, researchers, developers) on their own documents — not institutions. Local-first and BYO-LLM are non-negotiable product identity; consequently the near-term reachable market is bounded by users who already run an MCP-capable LLM. Breadth is pursued by **lowering setup friction** (multi-provider first-run wizard / roadmap D4), not by a bundled/hosted inference layer (which would add recurring cost and revisit local-first — deferred to a possible post-1.0 decision). *Note (2026-06-11, two amendments — canonical record in [ADR-039](#adr-039-non-mcp-model-providers-local-slice-v10-cloud-slice-v11)): (a) the multi-provider registry half moved to v1.1; (b) same day, the **local-model slice returned to v1.0** (Bryan, #1123). Net consequence: v1.0's reachable audience is Claude users plus anyone running a local model, and the "bounded by users who already run an MCP-capable LLM" framing above relaxes for the local path — the #1123 loop drives non-MCP local endpoints directly. Cloud BYO keys (OpenAI/Gemini) remain v1.1.* Supersedes positioning.md's §The Market and the institutional/technical-user audience framing recorded in **ADR-038's Context** ("gates the audience to developers and technical users" is ADR-038's phrasing). ADR-038's MCP-first integration *policy* is unaffected and is the basis for §2.

**Decision §2 — Moat: same canvas + persistent review record.** Headline: you and your AI work on the same live document — no copy-pasting between a chat window and your editor — highlighting text the AI sees and edits/comments on **in place**, as first-class objects you **accept, dismiss, or discuss**, powered by your own MCP LLM. The durable differentiator beneath the headline is annotations as **persistent, addressable, queryable first-class objects** + the **.docx review-record loop** (Word-comment round-trip). ChatGPT Canvas, Claude artifacts (MCP-connected), and `docx-mcp` do in-place editing, but not a persistent, queryable, exportable review record — that is the wedge. BYO-MCP-LLM is the enabler. Basis: ADR-038 (MCP-first).

**Decision §3 — Monetization & capture: free beta → one-time license at v1.0.** Free during public beta. At v1.0 **one public build** self-trials and **requires a valid offline-signed license to keep running** past the trial (a hard gate, not a nag); its auto-updates come from a **license-checked endpoint** that serves new builds only while the license's update window is current (this enforces a bundled-updates window / paid major-version upgrades). Pricing set later (~$29–79). *(Superseded by the 2026-10-17 amendment under §6, which records the price.)* No separate gated download — a shared installer is useless without a license, so license-to-run is the capture vector and gating the download would be redundant infra. The trial clock is on-device → soft; the hard gate is the signed license. *(Note 2026-06-11, #1123: the gate applies **identically** when the configured model is a free local LLM — bringing your own model does not change what's free; see ADR-039.)* Source-available remains a high-bar escape hatch. Existing beta users are **grandfathered** with a free signed license at 1.0 (narrowed 2026-11-02 to beta testers who sent feedback; see the amendment under Consequences) (goodwill over the small early-cohort revenue); new users pay. *Amendment 2026-10-09 (Bryan, decisions A5 and B6):* "tandem is a one-time purchase for a key with all future updates included in that purchase, but with no guarantee of future updates". So the update window and the paid major-version upgrades named above are dropped: every key, paid or grandfathered, is issued with a null update window (`expiresAt` / `updateWindowEnd`), and there is no renewal product (B6). The license-checked endpoint stays. With every issued window open-ended, what it refuses is an unknown id, a refunded key (which carries a `revoked` tombstone), or the expired window of a key hand-signed with `--expires`. `scripts/sign-license.ts` defaults to no window as well; `--expires` remains for tests and deliberate exceptions. The in-app "Renew" link and the dialog's renew instruction are gone. Bryan confirmed on 2026-10-09 that no key had been issued before this change, so none needs migrating.

**Decision §4 — Activation: offline signed license files.** *Running* validates an Ed25519-signed license on-device against an embedded public key — no network, no telemetry, air-gapped, binds a copy to its buyer. Update *checks* are network (as today); for the licensed build they authenticate entitlement at the update endpoint, which **logs only what's needed to authorize** (ideally a signed entitlement check, not the raw key). No usage analytics — the no-telemetry promise holds for running the app.

**Decision §5 — Licensing change (Accepted 2026-06-12; evaluation period amended 2026-09-08, #1909; resale sentence removed 2026-10-09, #2164).** Narrows the Additional Use Grant to permit personal use and individual self-hosting solely for evaluation purposes for ~~up to 30 days~~ **up to 14 days from first launch on a given device, for version 1.0.0 and later**. Every version released before 1.0.0, the public beta, stays free with no time limit. *Amendment 2026-09-08 (Bryan, #1909):* the 30-day figure never matched the product. The trial it gates has always been 14 days (§3, `TRIAL_DAYS`), so `LICENSE` was rewritten to match the enforcement, and this section now matches `LICENSE`. Continued or production use requires a paid commercial license. The Change Date resets per version (calculated as two years after the public general availability release of each specific version), ensuring commercial protection remains current across subsequent releases. *Note 2026-10-01 (license review):* `LICENSE` states the Change Date as "the earlier of 2029-06-10 or two years after the public general availability release of this specific version", so the per-version two-year clock is capped by the fixed date, which this section leaves out. The Context paragraph above has the date but pairs it with "v1.0 GA + 2 years", the wording that shipped in v0.11.0 through v0.14.1. What the cap means for versions released after 2027-06-10 is an open licensor decision; see [the 2026-10-01 license review](reviews/2026-10-01-license-review.md). *Amendment 2026-10-09 (Bryan, #2164):* the closing sentence "Commercial hosting or resale of the Licensed Work is not permitted." was removed from the Additional Use Grant. It read as a restriction on the base grant. The second of MariaDB's published Covenants of Licensor for BUSL 1.1 says an Additional Use Grant must not impose one; that section is not reproduced in `LICENSE` (review finding T2, #2132). Hosting for third parties stays outside the grant through its personal-use scope. Copies stay redistributable under the base grant, with the License displayed. A recipient's use is still limited to non-production use plus the Additional Use Grant. Bryan accepted that trade-off when he chose removal. *Amendment 2026-10-09 (Bryan, licensor decisions on the [2026-10-01 license review](reviews/2026-10-01-license-review.md)):* the `LICENSE` Parameters were rewritten and the stock BUSL text restored. Each change, by decision letter:
- **A0 (review L3):** the grant is recast as "You may make production use … provided that", with no "for evaluation" and without the draft's closing exclusion of hosting and selling.
- **A1 and L6:** "personal use" and "individual self-hosting" are defined in `LICENSE`, and the employee-exclusion sentence was not adopted, so employees are inside the grant.
- **A2:** no revocation clause and no transfer wording. The Licensor never revokes a key, even after a refund; a refund ends only the update entitlement (the `revoked` tombstone). The stock Terms' automatic termination on violation is unchanged.
- **A3:** production use is left undefined, on its ordinary reading.
- **A4 (review L4):** v1.0 and later may be used without a key, for personal use or individual self-hosting, provided that the use does not include the AI features. "AI features" is defined and names the MCP server, the chat panel and local-model support. This supersedes "Continued or production use requires a paid commercial license" above: after the 14 days a key the Licensor issued (paid or grandfathered, review L5) is needed for the AI features only.
- **A6 (review L2):** the Change Date is four years from the date each version is first made publicly available. For versions released under this wording, this replaces "two years after the public general availability release" and the 2029-06-10 cap (versions already released keep the Change Date their own `LICENSE` states), and supersedes the 2026-10-01 note above. It equals BUSL's own four-year limit, so neither runs first.
- **A7 (review L1, #1908):** Licensed Work names the repository and every artifact built from it and distributed by the Licensor (npm package, desktop app, Claude Code plugin), excluding third-party components, and ties a version to a published release. Pinning the plugin marketplace entry's `ref` is the build half (#2173).
- **A8 (review T1–T3):** the current MariaDB header and Notice sentence, the closing permission paragraph and the Covenants of Licensor are restored.
- **A9 (review C5, C8):** the beta grant names personal use and individual self-hosting, and also applies to every pre-1.0 version released under an earlier wording, including the v0.14.2–v0.25 releases that offered only a 30-day evaluation. Bryan: "eval period is 14 days but only applies once we hit 1.0".
- **A11 (review L9):** a contact line for other licensing arrangements, support@tandem.ink.
- **L7:** the Change License is named "MIT License (SPDX identifier: MIT)".
- **Decision C2 (review C8):** the v0.11.0–v0.14.3 desktop builds whose metadata said MIT are accepted as MIT.

The trial clock in §3 is unchanged and now matches the licence's own wording: 14 days from first launch, counted once across versions. The update-window decision (A5) is recorded as an amendment at the end of §3.

**Decision §6 — Distribution & payment.** Checkout via a Merchant of Record (Polar.sh or Paddle) for payment + global VAT/sales-tax + the issuance webhook; licensing decoupled, low lock-in. **One public build** stays on GitHub Releases; the licensed app's updater authenticates entitlement at a small license-checked endpoint (Keygen, or a Cloudflare Worker). License-to-run is enforced in the **server** (booted by both the Tauri sidecar and the npm CLI). LLC + accountant before taking money. *Amendment 2026-10-09 (Bryan, A10):* no company will be formed ("i wont be forming a company"). Bryan stays the Licensor and sells as an individual, with Polar as merchant of record, so the LLC half of the sentence above is withdrawn. Whether an accountant is wanted before the first sale was not decided, and stays with pricing in `docs/roadmap.md`.

*Amendment 2026-10-17 (Bryan): Paddle, not Polar, is the merchant of record.* This supersedes "with Polar as merchant of record" in the 2026-10-09 amendment above and the 2026-07-03 Polar narrowing under Consequences. The reasons: comparable one-time-licence desktop tools sell through Paddle or FastSpring; Paddle was checked to accept sole traders, which matters because no company will be formed (A10); and its fee is the same (5% + 50¢ per transaction). The issuance Worker was ported to Paddle Billing in the same change: it verifies `Paddle-Signature` (HMAC-SHA256 over `ts:body`), issues on `transaction.completed`, looks the buyer up with a `customer.read` API key (Paddle's webhook carries no email), and handles refunds and chargebacks from `adjustment.*` events. Polar's svix verification is gone, not kept beside it. Price $45; a student code takes $25 off. Partial refunds keep the licence and leave the operator a notice (2026-10-18). The port has not run against a real Paddle account; the unverified facts and the deploy gate are in `docs/licensing-operations.md` §8.

**Options considered:**
- **Public binary + honor-system nag (one-time, whole app)** — no capture vector; rejected.
- **Gated download host + dual trial/paid builds** — redundant with license-to-run; extra infra for no added capture; rejected.
- **Subscription / hosted SaaS** — contradicts local-first/no-backend; rejected.
- **Enterprise / support contracts** — mismatched to individuals; rejected.
- **Donations / free forever (go-light)** — lower-effort fallback; rejected — full commitment to the paid model.
- **Online license validation for running** — phones home; rejected for offline files (§4).

**Consequences:**
- Doc surfaces updated: positioning.md, README.md (free-during-beta + activation/telemetry + audience bullets), roadmap.md (#394), security.md, workflows.md + user-guide.md, CHANGELOG.md.
- One public build throughout (no installer takedown); the only gated surface is the update endpoint.
- In-app license-verification + server-side trial gate + license-authenticated updater are v1.0 work — tracked in #1116 (engineering, Wave 5L / v0.16.0) + #1117 (commercial infra, Bryan-led calendar gate) since 2026-06-11; the v1.0.0 tag is gated by the Commercial-readiness exit criterion in roadmap.md.
- Existing beta users are grandfathered with a free license at 1.0; new users pay. *Amended 2026-11-02: the free license goes to beta testers who sent feedback during the beta, contacted directly (licensing-operations.md §1c). Tandem has no list of beta users, so "every beta user" could not be honoured as written.*
- §1/§2 finalized; revenue ceiling is modest and accepted (full commitment, no kill-criterion).
- *Superseded 2026-10-17 (see the §6 amendment above): the seam is Paddle-only now.* **§6 narrowed to Polar for the issuance seam (2026-07-03, #1176):** the issuance Worker (`infra/license-issuance-worker/`) verifies Polar's Standard-Webhooks (svix) signature scheme specifically — it does not implement Paddle's webhook scheme. §6's "Polar.sh or Paddle" framing is still open at the checkout-provider level, but the built issuance seam is Polar-only in practice; adding Paddle later means a second signature-verification path in the Worker, not a drop-in swap.


**Amendment (2026-08-18, #1346) — unlicensed is a plain markdown editor: a surface gate, not a per-tool one.**

Five decisions (@bloknayrb). §3's *strictness* is unchanged — it was always a hard gate. What changes is the **shape** of what falls inside it, and one of the five is not a licensing decision at all.

**1. Unlicensed = plain markdown editor, no AI integration at all.** Not a read-only AI. The gate becomes a **surface** gate — refuse the AI surfaces outright — rather than the content-write, per-tool gate that is merged today (twelve `gatedTool()` MCP tools plus their `/api` twins, with reads, chat, `open`, save/export and `tandem_resolveAnnotation` explicitly Allowed). Two reasons beyond the commercial one: a twelve-tool enumeration must be re-audited on every new tool and Critical Rule 9 exists because forgetting one half of a pair is a silent hole, whereas a surface refusal has one thing to get right; and "Tandem without a license is a markdown editor" is a sentence a user can hold, where "Claude can read but not annotate" is not.

**Surface A stops gating document rooms.** Today `applyConnectionGate` marks every non-`CTRL_ROOM` connection read-only when restricted. Under this amendment that is close to the inverse of the intent: unlicensed means *your document is fully editable, there is simply no AI*. The `CTRL_ROOM` carve-out inverts with it — `CTRL_ROOM` is kept writable today so chat survives, and chat is now an AI surface that must not.

**2. Annotation resolution is a user action. This is a feature requirement, not a gating carve-out.** Accept and reject belong to the editor UI; the user can resolve, Claude cannot. It holds at **every** license status, licensed included, so it is not conditional on the gate and does not ship dark with it.

*Verified at master.* The user half is **already native** and needs no work: `useAnnotationReview.svelte.ts` `resolveAnnotation()` writes the status straight into `Y.Map('annotations')` and applies any `suggestedText` client-side through ProseMirror, over Hocuspocus. It touches neither MCP nor `/api` — no route exists and the client calls none. The remaining work is therefore **subtractive, not constructive**: retire `tandem_resolveAnnotation` (`src/server/mcp/annotations.ts`), which is wrapped in `withErrorBoundary` and is deliberately ungated today, along with the `store.acceptAnnotation` / `dismissAnnotation` lifecycle entries where they exist only to serve it. *(Not carried out: the third amendment, D4, keeps `tandem_resolveAnnotation` pending re-confirmation in #2201.)*

This also **independently confirms the Surface A change above**. Because the user's accept is a Hocuspocus write, a Surface A read-only clamp would block the user from resolving their own annotations — precisely the action this decision makes theirs alone. The two decisions would contradict each other if Surface A kept gating documents.

**3. One enforcement point across every AI surface — OPEN; discovery completed 2026-08-18, design pending.** *(Decided and built: see the third amendment, ¶2.)* The requirement is that MCP transport, Surface A and the `/api` routes share a single enforcement point. *Verified at master: they do not.* The requirement is also **ambiguous**, and the two readings have opposite answers: one decision *function* already exists, while one *interception* is not reachable by refactor, because the surfaces share no call path — a WebSocket connection lifecycle, JSON-RPC dispatch, Express middleware and an in-process call cannot be routed through one frame. They share a decision **primitive** — `resolveLiveLicenseState()`, with both consumers branching on `status === "restricted"` — but enforcement is spread across five sites — *mechanisms*, not surfaces; see the surface count below — each deciding independently what to do, and rendering refusal three different ways (an MCP error envelope, an HTTP 403, and a silent read-only connection clamp):

1. `gatedTool()` — registration-site wrapper, twelve MCP tools.
2. Direct in-handler `licenseGate()` — `src/server/mcp/document.ts` and `routes/open.ts` (the `force: true` sub-path). Not discoverable by grepping the wrapper.
3. `licenseGateMiddleware` — seven mutating `/api` routes, at the registration site.
4. `applyConnectionGate` — Surface A, Hocuspocus `onAuthenticate`.
5. `licenseGate()` inside `src/server/local-model/tools.ts` — **the local-model collaborator loop (ADR-039, #1123), which the issue's inventory does not name.** Its own comment states it "bypasses both license enforcement surfaces (no Hocuspocus connection; not an MCP tool)". It is an AI surface, so under decision 1 the whole loop is refused when unlicensed, not merely its three mutating tools.

`deriveLicenseUi` in the client is a sixth, advisory site; it must not be counted as enforcement.

Two surfaces have no enforcement site at all today because the current shape does not gate them, and acquire one under decision 1: **chat** (`CTRL_ROOM`, `tandem_reply`, `tandem_checkInbox`), and the **AI wake/push paths** (`/api/wake`, the channel shim, the plugin monitor, the supervisor), which exist solely to wake a model. Collapsing all of this is the actual design work this issue now owns.

**The surface inventory (discovery, 2026-08-18).** Counting by *surface* rather than by mechanism gives **six**, and that is the axis this decision asks about — which is why the question could not be answered as posed: nothing enumerated the surfaces, the gated-set list being an inventory of *operations*. It is now enumerated in [`docs/licensing-explained.md`](licensing-explained.md#ai-surfaces--the-1346-inventory) and pinned by `tests/docs/ai-surface-inventory-claims.test.ts`, which derives its sets from source so a new AI surface fails the build rather than going unlisted. Three results change the work:

- **MCP over stdio is free.** `src/cli/mcp-stdio.ts` registers no handlers and forwards raw JSON-RPC, so refusing at the HTTP transport also refuses Claude Desktop and Cowork. There is no fourth transport to gate.
- **Chat and the push paths are deliberately open, not overlooked.** `connectionShouldBeReadOnly` exempts `CTRL_ROOM` and `shouldForwardExternally` passes `chat:message` unconditionally, both so chat survives as the read-only escape hatch. Decision 1 inverts that: chat *is* the AI integration.
- **Surface A is not an AI surface at all.** It clamps the human's editor — the same conclusion decision 2 reaches by the annotation-resolution route, so two independent paths arrive at deleting it.

**Four admission points, not one interception.** Each surface has one narrow admission point, and three are existing functions with every caller in one place: `appendClaudeChatMessage()` (every AI chat write), `subscribe(cb, "external")` (every push consumer), the MCP session handshake (both transports), and the collaborator's start path. Four checks against the 20+ per-operation checks today, taking Critical Rule 9's per-tool cost from two edits to zero — the strongest *technical* argument for decision 1, independent of the commercial one.

**One consequence, settled by decision 7 below:** the sixteen ungated read tools stop being safely ungated. They are ungated on the rule that reads are the escape hatch, and `RESTRICTED_MESSAGE` promises it verbatim — but under decision 1 the escape hatch belongs to the **human's editor**: the user keeps opening, reading and exporting, and the AI reads nothing. Open question 1 in #1346 settled what happens to existing annotations but not whether Claude may still *read* them; the second amendment answers that it may not.

**4. No fail-open, no grace period.** Licenses are offline-verified and perpetual: the run gate checks the Ed25519 signature only, enforced at the type level by the `SignatureVerified` brand so that wiring in the stricter expiry-checking verifier is a compile error. There is no expiry to fail open from and nothing to specify — ADR-040 as accepted contains no fail-open or grace-period language, and this amendment introduces none. *(The "no grace period" half is superseded by the 2026-10-28 amendment (#2231): a licence that is installed but fails its check keeps the AI on for 14 days from its first failure. The rest stands: a verifying licence still runs forever, and nothing fails open on an expiry.)*

One precision, so "no expiry" is not over-read: it is a property of **licenses**, not of the trial. The on-device trial clock does end (`TrialInfo.expiresAt`), and a license's own `expiresAt` governs the **update window** alone — never whether the app runs.

**5. Terminology: always "unlicensed", never "expired" or "lapsed".** There are no lapsed users, only people who never bought. This is copy, and the copy that contradicts it is already written: `RESTRICTED_MESSAGE` in `src/server/mcp/license-gate.ts` opens "Your Tandem trial has ended", and the wall, the trial banner and that module's header comment all frame the state as a trial ending. All of it is rewritten at the flip to name the state as unlicensed and the consequence as AI features disabled. *(Done early: #1521 rewrote it while the gate was still dark. Third amendment.)* The internal `status: "restricted"` identifier is **not** required to change — `license-types.ts` records that both gates decide on that exact literal, so a fourth status value or a rename fails **open** at every enforcement surface until all of them are updated. *(2026-10-28, #2231: the grace period adds exactly such a fourth value, `grace`, on purpose — failing open there is the point. `restricted` stays the only refusing literal, in `aiRefusalFor` and in the desktop's `update_route`.)*

**Second amendment (2026-08-19, #1346) — the trial, the toolset, and what unlicensed looks like.** Bryan's answers to the three questions the first amendment left open, recorded as four decisions (6–9: the fourth is the copy the other three imply). Still dark; still no code moves here.

**6. The trial survives, and it is not in tension with decision 5.** Fourteen days of **full** functionality with no purchase, after which a license key is required to continue with full functionality *(that is, with the AI features; the editor stays usable without a key, third amendment ¶1)*. `TrialInfo` and the on-device clock stay exactly as built. The apparent conflict with "there are no lapsed users" dissolves on inspection: **a trial ending is not a license expiring.** Someone whose fourteen days ran out never bought a license, so nothing of theirs lapsed and nothing was taken — "unlicensed" describes them accurately. Decision 5 governs how the state is *named*, not whether a clock exists. So the clock is mechanism and stays; "expired" is vocabulary and goes.

**7. Unlicensed means Claude holds no `tandem_*` tools at all — reads included.** This closes the half of open question 1 the issue did not ask. The issue settled that existing annotations stay visible and resolvable *by the user*; it left open whether Claude may still read them. It may not. There is no partial toolset and no read-only AI mode: the surface gate refuses the MCP handshake, so the tools are absent rather than present-and-failing. *(Corrected by the third amendment, ¶3: that holds at the handshake. A session already open at expiry keeps its tool list, and each call then returns `-32003`. The channel shim's own `tandem_reply` stays listed, D2.)*

**Absent tools move the refusal, and the refusal must move with them.** `RESTRICTED_MESSAGE` reaches a model today only as a `gatedTool` `mcpError` or an HTTP 403 body — both of which require a tool to call. Refusing at the handshake deletes every one of those delivery paths, so the string has to be carried by the **HTTP MCP transport's own refusal**, at the handshake. Precisely there and nowhere else: `mcp-stdio.ts` synthesizes `-32000` for *upstream unavailability* ("the HTTP server never came up or died mid-session"), which is a different failure with different copy — a licence refusal routed through it would tell a Cowork user their server is unreachable, which is the no-explanation condition this paragraph exists to prevent. The bridge **relays** the upstream refusal verbatim, as a proxy; it must not synthesize one. *(Corrected by the third amendment, ¶3: relaying needed real code, and only an HTTP 200 carrying a JSON-RPC error passes through.)* This is not a detail: #1463 measured that a session with no `tandem_*` tools and no explanation is exactly the condition under which the model edits the file directly, and the bundled skill's absent-tools section is written against a refusal that says why.

Two consequences worth stating because they are easy to get backwards:

- **The escape hatch belongs to the editor, not to Claude.** "You can always open, read and export your work" remains true and is the point — but it is the *user* doing it. The sixteen ungated read tools are not a carve-out to preserve; they disappear with the surface.
- **`mcp-stdio.ts` needs no gate of its own.** It registers no handlers and forwards raw JSON-RPC, so refusing the HTTP transport also refuses Claude Desktop and Cowork. Verified during the discovery step; do not add a second refusal there. *(Still no second licence decision there, but mid-session the bridge now latches the server's refusal and answers requests with it locally; third amendment, ¶3.)*

**8. The unlicensed UI: frozen transcript, with a persistent notice.** The chat/annotation rail keeps its footprint and its **history stays full-colour and readable**; only the surfaces that would *reach Claude* — the chat composer and the annotation-creation affordances — grey out, alongside a persistent notice naming the state.

**Accept and reject stay live.** Decision 2 holds them at every license status, licensed included, and it is not overridden here: they are the user's own action on their own document, they are a Hocuspocus write rather than an AI call, and greying them would strand annotations the user already has with no way to clear them. Only *creating* new AI work is refused, never resolving existing work. Rationale: the failure this design most needs to avoid is a user believing their work was taken, and keeping the transcript at full fidelity makes "we did not take anything" a visible fact rather than a claim in a support document. The notice exists because a greyed composer alone is too subtle a boundary — a user discovers the state by trying to type.

Two rejected alternatives, recorded because both are reasonable and will be re-proposed:

- **Collapsing the rail to a spine** is correct as a collapse the **user chooses**, and wrong as the default. Someone who has decided not to buy should be able to reclaim the width permanently; deciding it for them on day 15 both hides the state and removes their choice.
- **Turning the rail into a permanent activation panel** is rejected as a *standing* state. Activation belongs in Settings and in the in-trial notice, not pinned beside every document indefinitely — that is the version of this that reads as nagging on day 400.

**The full-screen wall goes, and `deriveLicenseUi` inverts.** As merged, `restricted` returns `editable: false, showWall: true` (`src/client/utils/license-ui.ts`), which is the *old* shape: a read-only document behind a blocking wall. Under decision 1 that is backwards twice over — the document stays editable, and the wall's own copy ("continue in read-only mode, where your documents stay open for reading, exporting, and chatting with Claude") promises exactly the read-only-AI product decision 7 refuses. So `restricted` becomes `editable: true, showWall: false`, and the trial-ended wall's job is taken by the rail notice plus Settings. This is the single largest client-side consequence of the amendment and the easiest to miss, because nothing in the per-tool gate's own surface area points at it.

**But `licenseUnverifiable` is a different person with a different problem, and must not be collapsed into it.** `LicenseWall.svelte` has two branches, and only the trial-ended one is retired. The other ("Your license needs attention") is shown to someone who **paid** and whose license file no longer verifies — a build mismatch, a corrupted file, a failed write. Routing them to the unlicensed rail notice would tell a paying customer to buy a license, which is the single worst string this system could produce; ADR-040's own history records that showing "your trial ended" to a beta tester who never had a trial was the bug that motivated the split-status copy. So `licenseUnverifiable` keeps a distinct, blocking surface with its own copy, it keeps `unverifiableLicenseMessage()`, and it says *fix or reissue*, never *purchase*. *(Superseded by the third amendment, D3: the wall is retired in both branches and the unverifiable holder's surface is non-blocking. The fix-or-reissue copy and the never-purchase rule stand. `editable` and `showWall`, above, no longer exist.)*

**9. Refusal copy.** Decision 5 applied. **Every refusal carries the reason *and* `TANDEM_PURCHASE_URL`** — #1463 measured a model inventing a remedy that does not exist when given neither, and today's `RESTRICTED_MESSAGE` already interpolates the URL, so dropping it would be a regression rather than a simplification. *(Except where the reader most likely paid or the URL cannot fit: an unverifiable refusal never carries it (decision 8, D3), the in-app AI-off notice points to Settings → License instead, and the wake socket's close carries only the code `LICENSE_REQUIRED`. Third amendment.)* `constants.ts`'s docblock enumerates five surfaces, but **six files reference the constant** — the docblock omits `SettingsLicenseTab.svelte`, which decision 8 makes the *primary* activation surface now that the wall is gone. The checklist is therefore `grep -rl TANDEM_PURCHASE_URL src/`, not the docblock; fix the docblock in the same change.

| Surface | Replaces | With |
|---|---|---|
| MCP transport refusal + HTTP 403 (now `AI_REFUSAL_UNLICENSED_MESSAGE`, `src/shared/license-copy.ts`; the unverifiable kind gets `aiRefusalUnverifiableMessage()`, no URL, and the fail-closed catch arm gets the licence-neutral `AI_REFUSAL_STATE_UNREADABLE_MESSAGE`, also no URL (#2206); a refused `initialize` also appends `AI_REFUSAL_RECONNECT_HINT`) | "Your Tandem trial has ended, so Tandem's editing and annotation tools are unavailable. Reading, opening and exporting still work, and the document itself is untouched. Activate a license to re-enable editing: {url}" | "Tandem is unlicensed, so its AI features are turned off. Your document is unaffected and stays fully editable. Activate a license to turn them back on: {url}" |
| Local-model loop (now `collaborator.ts`, `AI_REFUSAL_LOCAL_MODEL_UNLICENSED_MESSAGE`) | "Editing is unavailable — the Tandem trial has ended; activate a license to keep editing." | "Tandem is unlicensed, so its AI features are turned off. Activate a license to turn them back on: {url}" |
| `tandem license` (`src/cli/license.ts`) | "Status: restricted — trial ended; activate a license to keep editing" | "Status: unlicensed — AI features off; the editor is unaffected", then a separate "Buy a license: {url}" line. Unverifiable: "Status: license not verified — AI features off; the editor is unaffected" and the fix-or-reissue paragraph, no URL. On a build with the gate dark: "no license key" / "license not verified" "— nothing is enforced in this build" (with "(trial clock: n of 14 days)" while the clock runs), no URL *(as shipped; third amendment)* |
| Full-screen wall (`LicenseWall.svelte`) | "Your 14-day trial is over. Activate a license to keep editing — or continue in read-only mode, where your documents stay open for reading, exporting, and chatting with Claude." | *(retired — see decision 8; the wall does not survive the reshape)* |
| Settings status pill (`deriveLicenseUi` `statusLabel`) | "Trial ended" | "Unlicensed" (unverifiable: "License not verified") |
| Rail notice (`AiOffNotice.svelte`) | *(none today)* | Title "AI features are off" (collapsed: "AI features off — unlicensed" / "— license not verified"); body "Tandem is unlicensed, so its AI features are off. Activate a license in Settings → License." (`aiOffCopy`, no URL). Unverifiable: the body is `aiRefusalUnverifiableMessage()` *(as shipped; third amendment)* |
| Settings → License tab (`SettingsLicenseTab.svelte`) | *(activation entry point; copy not trial-framed today)* | Primary activation surface once the wall is retired. Carries the reason and a "Buy a license" link to `{url}`, hidden for an unverifiable holder; the "Activate a license" form heading stays for both *(as shipped; third amendment, D3)*. |
| Trial banner (`LicenseBanner.svelte`) | "{n} of 14 days left in your Tandem trial." | Unchanged in shape — it is already neither "expired" nor "lapsed". Only the end-state copy moves. |

The second column quotes the **current** strings so the diff is checkable; `{url}` is `TANDEM_PURCHASE_URL`, never a literal.

The internal `status: "restricted"` literal is **not** renamed — `license-types.ts` records that both gates decide on that exact string, so a rename fails **open** at every enforcement surface until all of them move together.

**Implementation tracker: #1521.** Both amendments are design records; no code moves on either. The reshape — four admission points replacing the per-tool gate, the deletion of Surface A and `tandem_resolveAnnotation`, the UI and the copy — is tracked there, and it lands **dark**. *(Implemented dark by #2194, #2202 and #2207. Surface A was deleted; `tandem_resolveAnnotation` was kept, #2201. See the third amendment.)* Flipping `LICENSE_GATE_ENABLED` remains a separate v1.0 exit gate.

**Constraint — everything stays dark.** `LICENSE_GATE_ENABLED` remains `false` in `tsup.config.ts` and the build stays byte-identical with the flag off. This amendment is a design change to merged-but-inert code; no code moves on it here. The gated-set enumeration in [`docs/licensing-explained.md`](licensing-explained.md#the-gated-set) is superseded in **shape** by decision 1 but remains an accurate description of the code as merged, so it stands as Critical Rule 9's review surface until the surface gate is implemented. *(Both now overtaken: "byte-identical" is restated as behaviour-identical (D7), and the surface gate is implemented, so the gated-set section describes it. Third amendment.)*

**Third amendment (2026-10-13, #1521) — the surface gate as built.** #1521 implemented the two amendments above in three PRs (#2194 server, #2202 client, #2207 the skill and this record), still dark. Building it overturned or corrected parts of decisions 2, 3, 6, 7, 8 and 9, and did decision 5's rewrite before the flip rather than at it; each carries an inline marker pointing here. The D-numbers are the decisions Bryan approved on 2026-10-12 in #1521's plan.

**1. Policy.** Decision F (2026-09-06, "restricted mode is symmetric read-only", `docs/reviews/2026-09-02-v1-review/decisions.md`) is superseded. Bryan, 2026-10-11: "yes, superseded. after the 14 day trial, tandem remains a usable editor, but with no AI features". That restores first-amendment decision 1 and second-amendment decision 6. The quote describes what the product does, not who may use it. Under `LICENSE` (Additional Use Grant, decision A4), version 1.0.0 and later may be used without a key for personal use or individual self-hosting: with the AI features for the first 14 days from the first launch on a given device (counted once across all such versions), and without them from then on. Anyone outside that scope has neither, whatever the gate lets through: the gate does not check who the user is. In product copy, "unlicensed" means "holds no key", not "has no right to use"; `LICENSE` grants that person the editor. §3's "requires a valid offline-signed license to keep running past the trial" therefore now means keep running the **AI features**.

**2. Where the gate sits** (decision 3, decided). One decision function, `aiRefusal()` (`src/server/license/ai-refusal.ts`), admits on its first line while the gate is dark and otherwise returns a refusal with a `kind`: `unlicensed` or `unverifiable`. It is consulted at every point where an AI gets in:
- `POST /mcp`, before any session lookup, and `GET /mcp`;
- stdio server mode, refused at start;
- chat writes (`appendClaudeChatMessage`), `/api/channel-reply` and `/api/channel-permission(-verdict)` (the last two deleted in #1885, 2026-10-28);
- push fan-out (which also revokes live external subscribers), and the SSE and wake upgrades;
- the launcher's `startInternal` and `respawn`;
- each local-model run.

Surface A, `gatedTool()`, the seven `licenseGateMiddleware` mounts and the two in-handler checks are deleted: the browser's own write paths are not AI surfaces. Detail and the residuals: [`docs/licensing-explained.md`](licensing-explained.md#enforcement-one-decision-at-the-ais-admission-points).

**3. Decision 7 corrected: how a refusal reaches the model.** The MCP SDK client passes only an HTTP 200 carrying a JSON-RPC error through to the host as an `McpError`. What the model then sees was measured: at the handshake, Claude Code lists the failed server as `tandem (-32003): "<message>"`, the server's configured name followed by the code; mid-session, a tool call's error is the bare message, with no code and no `data`. A non-2xx `POST` becomes a transport error that embeds the raw body but loses the JSON-RPC code and `data`; a non-2xx `GET` keeps only the status text. So a refused `POST /mcp` answers **HTTP 200** with JSON-RPC error `-32003` (`LICENSE_REQUIRED_RPC_CODE`), the refusal copy as its `message`, and `data: { error: "LICENSE_REQUIRED", kind, purchaseUrl? }`. A refused `initialize` also carries a reconnect hint, because the host marks the server failed. `GET /mcp` and the `/api` routes answer 403 with the `{ error, kind, message }` envelope. The stdio bridge relays the server's error and never synthesizes its `-32000` for one. Mid-session it latches the refusal, answers requests with it locally, and retries the handshake every 30 s (`TANDEM_LICENSE_RETRY_MS` is a test seam only). It strips the reconnect hint from its own replay, because the host's session never failed. The latch is relay state, not a second licence decision. Tools are absent only for a session refused at its handshake. A session already open when the trial ends keeps its tool list, and each call returns the refusal.

**4. What was measured** (PR #2194, 2026-10-12, Claude Code `claude -p`, Sonnet, over HTTP and over the stdio bridge):
- A refused `initialize` shows the server as failed, and the model receives the `-32003` message.
- When the trial ends mid-session, the next tool call returns the licence message as its error.
- After the licence is restored, the same session heals: over HTTP on the next call, through the bridge within about 40 s.

**Interactive** (2026-10-13, Claude Code 2.1.296, Sonnet, over HTTP, against a dev build with the gate armed; one run per case):
- A refused `initialize` reaches the model as a system notice, `tandem (-32003): "<message> Once that is done, reconnect …"`. Claude Code cut the text at 200 characters (one sample, of the unlicensed kind). Applying that cut to the unverifiable messages (inferred, not observed): `BAD_SIGNATURE` loses its whole fix, the read-failure default loses part of it, and `UNSUPPORTED_VERSION` keeps it; the skill points to Settings → License for the whole message.
- When the trial ends mid-session, the tool error is the bare message: no `-32003`, no `LICENSE_REQUIRED`, no `data`. The `-p` runs above show the same, over HTTP and through the bridge.
- With skill v33, Claude relayed the message, pointed to Settings → License and did not edit the file, in both cases (measured). That it matched on the message's opening rather than the code is inferred: mid-session there was no code to match. Skill v34 keys the mid-session rule on the openings for that reason.

**Not measured:**
- a release build (#2204), and the unverifiable kind in a live session;
- whether a 403 body or the wake socket's close reason reach the model (`data` does not: see above);
- whether a host respawns the bridge after a refused first `initialize`;
- whether a launcher child running when the trial ends re-handshakes by itself after activation.

The shipped skill's remedy branches key on the measured carriers: the code where it arrives (the handshake), and how the message begins (everywhere, and the only key mid-session). It also reacts to unmeasured ones (a wake close naming `LICENSE_REQUIRED`, the shim's `tandem_reply` 403, the plugin monitor's line, a `license_refused` channel event), but only by telling the user once, without naming which case it is unless the message's own sentence says so.

**5. D3: a licence that fails to verify.** The editor stays editable and the AI is off. *(2026-10-28, #2231: for the first 14 days from the first failure the AI stays on; see the last amendment.)* A non-blocking fix-or-reissue notice appears, with no purchase link, and the user is never called "unlicensed". The full-screen wall is retired in **both** branches, superseding decision 8's "distinct, blocking surface"; the client keys every AI-off surface on one discriminant, `aiNotice`. The Settings → License form keeps its "Activate a license" heading for this user, because entering the key again is one of their remedies. The invariant is no purchase prompt and never "unlicensed", not "never the word Activate" (Bryan, 2026-10-13).

**6. D1: the launcher.** The supervisor refuses to spawn Tandem's Claude while the AI is refused. That covers boot, the launcher routes and crash-respawn, and a refused relaunch never stops a running child. A child already running when the trial ends is left alone, and Tandem refuses its calls and wakes. `POST /api/license/activate` resumes a refused launcher (`resumeAfterLicence`). **Gap:** a licence that becomes valid any other way does not resume it. That means `tandem activate` from the CLI, a hand-copied `license.json`, or activation after the user pressed Stop. The client offers Restart instead. Bryan chose to fix the CLI path in a separate PR, #2205 (2026-10-13). #2205 closed the CLI half: when a Tandem is running, `tandem activate` hands the key to this route, so it resumes the launcher like Settings does; a hand-copied `license.json` and activation after a user Stop remain gaps.

**7. D2.** The channel shim's own `tandem_reply` stays listed. Decision 7's "no `tandem_*` tools" therefore means none from Tandem's MCP server. While refused no chat events reach the shim, and a call it does make returns the refusal.

**8. D4.** `tandem_resolveAnnotation` is kept, not retired as decision 2 planned. The per-tool gate that partly motivated retiring it is gone, so retiring it now would affect only licensed users. Re-confirmation is #2201.

**9. D5: accepted residuals.** Four, plus two of the same kind, are recorded in [`docs/licensing-explained.md`](licensing-explained.md#enforcement-one-decision-at-the-ais-admission-points). The headline is that an unlicensed Claude Code with shell access can still reach Tandem's local endpoints directly, which is equivalent to editing the file on disk. They are licence-enforcement residuals, not security findings.

**10. D7: "byte-identical" restated as behaviour-identical when dark.** Deleting code changes bundle bytes, and byte equality was never tested. What holds:
- Admission is tested with the gate dark for `POST`/`GET /mcp`, SSE, the wake upgrade, chat, fan-out and stdio mode (`tests/server/license-ai-refusal.test.ts`). The launcher, the local model and the channel-permission routes (deleted in #1885) rely on the same `aiRefusal()` first line.
- The deleted code was already inert when dark.
- Two things do reach dark users, and both are made harmless. The shipped skill's licence instructions fire only on a licence refusal (`-32003`, a carrier naming `LICENSE_REQUIRED`, or an error beginning with one of the two refusal openings), which a dark build never sends, because `aiRefusal()` admits on its first line. `tandem license` on a dark build with no valid key says "nothing is enforced in this build" and never "unlicensed", "trial … remaining" or a purchase link.

#1521's other restated acceptance criteria are reconciled on the issue.

**Amendment (2026-10-15) — licenses name their signing key.** Bryan, relayed from tandem-website #59: "seems like there is no downside to adding the signing key before the first sale; you didn't need to get my input on that one so long as you documented it properly". Before this there was one pinned public key and no way to replace it without dropping every customer to `restricted`. A field inside the signed metadata can only be added before any paid key exists, because it changes the signed bytes.

- **What:** every license's metadata carries `keyId`, and the app trusts a list of public keys (`TRUSTED_PUBLIC_KEYS`, `src/server/license/public-key.ts`). The id is the key's own fingerprint (first 8 bytes of SHA-256 over the raw public key, in hex). Both signers derive it from the key they sign with, so a license cannot name a key other than its signer's. A license without `keyId` is checked against `LEGACY_KEY_ID`, the original key, looked up in the same list, so retiring that key retires keyless licenses too. *Superseded 2026-10-20: `LEGACY_KEY_ID` and the keyless fallback are gone; see the amendment below.*
- **Refusals:** the issuance Worker refuses (503, stage `config`) to sign with a key whose id is not in its `TRUSTED_KEY_IDS`, and a parity test keeps that list equal to the app's. `scripts/sign-license.ts` refuses an untrusted key the same way.
- **No new error code:** an id the build does not carry answers `BAD_SIGNATURE`, with its existing "may need to be reissued" copy. After a leak, reissue is the right remedy. For a planned rotation, the runbook ships the new key in releases before anything is signed with it, so a new-key license meeting an old build is rare and support can say "update". A separate "update Tandem" code was considered and dropped: a restricted device's in-app updater refuses it, so the copy would have pointed at a dead end.
- **Honest scope:** a plain list of keys with no id would have given the same security, since a license still verifies only if a trusted key signed it. The id adds a single verify per license and an unambiguous record of which key signed it, which the rotation procedure relies on. Builds already shipped verify a `keyId`-carrying license from the original key unchanged (they canonicalize every field); a license from any other key fails on them, and that cannot be fixed retroactively.
- **Procedure:** [`licensing-operations.md` §0](licensing-operations.md#rotating-the-signing-key). No rotation has run yet.

**Amendment (2026-10-20) — new signing key; the original retired as lost; licenses carry no name.** The private half of the original key, `79c10e75fb8cfa46` (#1133, 2026-06-13), was lost: it had been written to a gitignored `keys/` in a checkout nobody can find, and the key ceremony had never been done for it. No license was ever issued to a customer with it (the gate ships dark, nothing had been sold, no grandfather key had been sent), so it was removed rather than rotated, and a new key, `c24dd9f66f449a56`, was generated outside any checkout and kept in two offline copies. On the PII decision left open in [`licensing-operations.md` §0](licensing-operations.md#0-key-management-the-ceremony--do-this-once-and-it-freezes-more-than-the-key), Bryan: "drop it; email is enough".

- **What:** `TRUSTED_PUBLIC_KEYS` and the Worker's `TRUSTED_KEY_IDS` hold only `c24dd9f66f449a56`. `keyId` is required on `LicenseMetadata` (`IssuedLicenseMetadata` folded into it); `LEGACY_KEY_ID` is deleted. `name` leaves `LicenseMetadata`, the issuance Worker's ledger record, the Paddle lookup (which reads `data.email` only) and the license email's greeting; the status wire's dark-build `licenseeName` becomes `licenseeEmail`, and the UI and CLI show the email.
- **Keyless answers `BAD_SIGNATURE`, not `MALFORMED`:** enforcement is the same either way, but the only keyless licenses were signed by the retired key, so `BAD_SIGNATURE`'s "predates a signing-key change, have it reissued" copy is the true description. A tester may hold one; the list was never knowable.
- **`version` stays `"1.0"`:** no license carrying `name` was ever issued, so there is no old shape to distinguish. Builds already shipped ignore `name`'s absence (they canonicalize whatever fields arrive) but trust only the retired key, so a new-key license fails on them; hence the two gates in `licensing-operations.md` §8: the sandbox activation check runs on a build from `master`, and no grandfather or live key is sent until a release carrying the new key has shipped.
- **The issuance Worker rolls forward only past this change:** an older Worker treats a name-less ledger record as corrupt. A record that still carries a `name` parses on the new one (extra fields are tolerated) and re-signs without it.

**Amendment (2026-10-28, #2231, #1943) — a grace period for a key that fails its check, and local removal.** Still dark: everything below is inert until `LICENSE_GATE_ENABLED` flips, except removal, which works on every build as activation does.

Bryan, 2026-10-14, on a paying customer whose installed key fails its check: "always err in the customer's favor; if there is a key problem, put a persistent notification in tandem's notification tray telling them to contact support@tandem.ink about it but otherwise don't restrict them". Never restricting would make the gate an honour system (verification is the only check and the source is public, so any junk file would unlock the AI forever). Offered (A) never restrict or (B) a grace period, he chose **B**. This supersedes decision 4's "no grace period" and the third amendment's D3 for the first 14 days.

- **What:** a licence file that is present but fails its check (any `LicenseUnverifiableCode`: `BAD_SIGNATURE`, `UNSUPPORTED_VERSION`, `MALFORMED`, `UNKNOWN`, and the activation-only codes the union also carries) resolves to a new status, `grace`, for `LICENSE_GRACE_DAYS` = **14 days** from the first time this device saw that file fail. The length is the issue's "about 14 days". It is its own constant, equal to `TRIAL_DAYS` by choice.
- **Precedence:** licensed, then grace, then the trial clock, then restricted. Grace outranks a running trial, so a licence holder is not framed as a trial user; when grace ends with the trial still running, the device is back on the trial (with `licenseUnverifiable`), and after both it is `restricted` with the unverifiable refusal, which never carries the purchase URL (decision 8).
- **The AI stays on.** `aiRefusalFor` refuses on `status === "restricted"` only, so `grace` admits with no change to `ai-refusal.ts` or to any admission point. Decision 5's warning that a fourth status "fails open" is exactly why this is safe to add: failing open is the intent.
- **Updates keep flowing.** The desktop's `update_route` withholds updates by the name `restricted` only; `grace` carries no `licenseId`, so it lands on the public manifest, like a trial. No Rust change. This also unblocks `UNSUPPORTED_VERSION`, whose remedy is "update Tandem", during the grace period. After grace ends that device is `restricted` and back on no updates, so `UNSUPPORTED_VERSION` past day 14 still has the dead end the 2026-10-15 amendment noted; closing it needs a Rust change and is out of scope.
- **Where the notice appears:** a `warning` in the activity tray (persistent: only `info` expires; best-effort, since the tray's history cap can still evict it, and it returns on the next page load), a notice in Settings → License, `tandem license`, and `tandem doctor`'s licence row (a warning, with the AI reported on). **Not** in Claude's tool results or any AI-facing surface: nothing new reaches `appendClaudeChatMessage`, an `"external"` subscriber or a tool result. Every grace notice names support@tandem.ink and none carries the purchase URL.
- **Storage:** `license-grace.json` beside `trial.json` (`{ version: 1, entries: { key: firstFailedAtIso } }`, owner-only, written by a temp file and a rename, at most 8 entries). The key is the SHA-256 of the licence file's stored `blob` when the file parses, of its raw bytes (a bounded prefix plus the length) when it does not, and the constant `read-error` when the file cannot be read at all, so a file made permanently unreadable still runs out. The live state resolver (`resolveLiveLicenseState`) writes it: every AI admission and every `GET /api/license/status` (the activate and deactivate responses included), so on an armed build the grace clock starts at the first of either, a Settings status poll included; `tandem license` reads it on an armed build only; a dark build never reads or writes it. A stored date before 2020 is ignored and replaced, as for `trial.json`. One more than a day ahead of now is ignored for the moment but, unlike `trial.json`'s, **never replaced**: the likelier cause is a clock that has fallen behind (a CMOS reset to a firmware date), and rewriting the real date with that clock's "now" would end a paying customer's grace the moment the clock is corrected. While the clock is behind, the grace runs from the behind clock's "now", remembered in memory for the process; a usable date on disk always wins over that memory. A verifying licence clears it, so a much later, unrelated failure (a transient lock) gets a fresh grace rather than an immediate refusal. Deactivation clears it too.
- **It is no stronger than the trial clock, stated plainly, and does not meet one of #2231's requirements.** The issue asks that "deleting Tandem's own state file must not reset the first-failure date". This design deliberately does not meet that: deleting `license-grace.json` resets it. The decision for this change was to store the date beside `trial.json` and accept the trial clock's strength, because with all state local and the source public, any file or registry entry Tandem writes is one a user can find and delete; hiding it elsewhere would only be obscurity, and a reset that is hard to find is also hard for support to undo. The one reset it does hold against is deleting `license.json` and putting the same bytes back, which keeps the first-failure date because the record lives in its own file and is keyed by the licence's contents. It does **not** hold against deleting `license-grace.json` itself, against cycling distinct junk licence files (each is a new key, a fresh 14 days), against removing a licence and copying the same broken file back, against a clock set behind the recorded date (each process runs the grace from its own "now" until the clock is right), or against an app-data directory the record cannot be written to (each process remembers the date in memory, so the grace is bounded for that process's life, but a restart reads day 0 again). Each of these re-grants a grace exactly as deleting `trial.json` re-grants a trial, and a write failure fails **open** on purpose: it must never refuse a paying customer. With all state local and the source public, no design can do better; a single device-level first-failure timestamp, cleared only when a licence verifies, would close the junk-file cycling and is left to Bryan.
- **Removal (#1943):** `tandem deactivate` and Settings → License → Remove license delete `license.json` and the grace record. **Local only:** no seat is released and nothing is sent. `trial.json` is untouched, so a device past its trial lands in `restricted`. Reversible by activating the same key from the email it came in, **except** for a key that already fails `BAD_SIGNATURE` or `UNSUPPORTED_VERSION` on this build, which activation refuses too; removing it also ends its remaining grace, so the confirm copy and the CLI's result say so instead of promising a reactivation. A Claude already running is left alone, as at trial expiry (D1); its next admission is decided by the live state. On a dark build the status wire's additive, loopback-only `licenseFileUnverifiable` flag lets Settings offer removal for a file that would not verify (a pre-rotation beta key), since the desktop app has no CLI, and hide the buy link beside it; like `licenseInstalled`, it is never a gate signal. The declined file picker in the activation form (#1789) is unchanged.

**Cross-references:** ADR-038 (MCP-first policy — basis for §2), ADR-022 / ADR-026 / ADR-027 (annotation system / authorship / data model — the in-place review surface), ADR-028 (split-status pattern), ADR-039 (local-model collaborator — the fifth enforcement site named in the 2026-08-18 amendment), `docs/positioning.md`, `docs/licensing-explained.md`, `docs/roadmap.md` #394 + D4, `LICENSE` (BUSL-1.1), #1116 (engineering tracker), #1346 (the 2026-08-18 amendment and the 2026-08-19 second amendment), #1521 (surface-gate implementation tracker).

## ADR-041: Customizable Keyboard Shortcuts (Override Layer)

**Status:** Accepted (2026-05-27)
**Context:** Tandem ships ~60 keyboard shortcuts, none user-configurable. The real key→action mapping is NOT the action registry (ADR-029 made `shortcut` display-only) — it lives in `matchShortcut()` (`src/client/hooks/useAppShortcuts.ts`), a hand-ordered `if/else` chain keyed on `e.code`/`e.key` + modifiers, with deliberately preserved "legacy quirks" (e.g. `Ctrl+S` matches even with Alt held; `Ctrl+Shift+S`→save-as is tested *before* `Ctrl+S`→save and falls through). App.svelte's dispatch table owns the side effects. Several matcher `ShortcutId`s carry runtime context via Shift or are digit families (`Ctrl+1..9`) and are not single discrete chords.
**Decision:** Add an **override layer** rather than rewriting the matcher. (1) Scope: only the App-level discrete (single-chord) shortcuts are remappable — the set is `REMAPPABLE_SHORTCUT_IDS` in `src/client/actions/keybindings.ts`, which is authoritative (20 ids as of 2026-09-23: `save`, `save-as`, `focus-chat`, `toggle-source-view`, `settings`, `toggle-palette`, `new-scratchpad`, `close-tab`, `open-file`, `toggle-mode`, `reopen-closed-tab`, `comment-on-selection`, `toggle-authorship`, `toggle-left-panel`, `toggle-right-panel`, `annotation-next`, `annotation-prev`, `select-block`, `new-tab-menu`, `toggle-formatting-bar`). Text-formatting/Tiptap keymaps and family/context shortcuts (`find`, `find-nav`, accept/dismiss, `pick-tab`, `toggle-help`, `select-all`) stay fixed/read-only. (2) `matchShortcut(e, overrides?)` loops overrides first (strict-equality `chordMatches`, so iteration order is not a correctness risk) and returns the remapped id; then runs the existing chain, but each *remappable* branch's return is wrapped `if (!isOverridden(id, overrides)) return …` so a remapped-away default still **falls through** to its sibling (remap `save-as` away ⇒ `Ctrl+Shift+S` falls through to `save`) instead of dying. Empty/undefined overrides ⇒ byte-identical to before, so non-remappers and the E2E suite see zero change. (3) Edit UX = click-to-record; conflicts block the assignment and name the owner. `findConflict` (`src/client/actions/shortcut-conflicts.ts`) checks effective remappable bindings, then **fixed matcher branches derived live from the matcher** (`claimedByFixedShortcut` synthesizes an event and runs `matchShortcut` with no overrides — see hardening note), then a `RESERVED_CHORDS` set covering only the **non-matcher** reservations (separate tab-cycle/zoom window listeners, version-pinned Tiptap *letter* keymaps). (4) Storage: `customShortcuts: Record<RemappableShortcutId, ShortcutChord>` in `TandemSettings` (schema v8→v9); `normalizeKnownFields` re-validates on every load/merge via `parseCustomShortcuts`, dropping entries that are junk, non-bindable (no primary modifier / Numpad / Tab-Escape-Enter), collide with a fixed branch or reserved chord, or duplicate a chord already held by a higher-priority id — so a stale or hand-edited override can't shadow a fixed shortcut or silently dead-bind via the override-first loop.
**Why not bind from registry strings:** the registry `shortcut` is a human label (`"Ctrl+Shift+S"`), not a machine chord, and registry ids don't map 1:1 to matcher `ShortcutId`s (`find` vs `find-in-tabs`/`find-next`; `annotation-previous` vs `annotation-prev`). The matcher is the binding authority; the registry crosswalk (`REGISTRY_TO_SHORTCUT_ID`) exists only for Help-catalog reflection.
**Consequences:** `chord` uses physical `e.code` (layout-independent, mirroring the matcher). `comment-on-selection` (Ctrl+Alt+M) and `toggle-palette` have no registry row, so they appear in the editable Settings list but not the Help catalog. The Tiptap reserved slice is hand-maintained and pinned to the `@tiptap/*` versions in `package.json` — a Tiptap major bump requires re-auditing it. New remappable shortcuts must be added to `REMAPPABLE_SHORTCUT_IDS` (append — its order is the dedupe priority between two overrides on one chord, so a new id should not outrank existing ones; an override beating a *default* is the override-first loop's job, independent of order), `DEFAULT_BINDINGS`, `REMAPPABLE_LABELS`, `REGISTRY_TO_SHORTCUT_ID` (if a registry action exists), the `ShortcutId` union, a matcher branch wrapped in `isOverridden`, and an App.svelte dispatch entry; new *fixed* shortcuts are picked up automatically by `claimedByFixedShortcut` if they go through `matchShortcut`, and only need a `RESERVED_CHORDS` entry if they live in a separate listener (tab-cycle/zoom) or a Tiptap keymap.
**Conflict-model hardening (2026-05-27):** the original design hand-transcribed the matcher's fixed chords into `RESERVED_CHORDS` as *exact tuples*, but several fixed branches match *families* (`find`/`find-nav` ignore Alt; `pick-tab` ignores Alt+Shift; the `?` help branch has no modifier gate). The exact-tuple list missed those variants, so a user could remap onto e.g. `Ctrl+Shift+/`, `Ctrl+Alt+F`, or `Ctrl+Shift+3` and the override-first loop would silently steal the fixed function. Fixed by deriving fixed-branch conflicts live from the matcher (`claimedByFixedShortcut` reuses `matchShortcut` as the single source of truth — no second copy of the gating to drift), moving the conflict/validation helpers into `shortcut-conflicts.ts` to avoid a circular import, and removing the now-redundant matcher entries from `RESERVED_CHORDS`. The one irreducible US-layout assumption is the synthetic `e.key` derivation for `?`/`/`; non-US layouts keep the matcher's pre-existing layout quirk. Because the feature is unshipped, the only holders of an exploit-bound override are pre-merge testers, who lose that binding on next load (restoring the fixed shortcut) — there is no public migration.
**`toggle-formatting-bar` (2026-09-23):** joined the set with default `Ctrl+Alt+F`, a chord #1777 had freed from `find`. This knowingly adds a fourth AltGr collision to Ctrl+Alt+T/M/A: AltGr+F types `[` on hu/cs/sk/hr/sl/bs/sr-Latin wherever the platform delivers AltGr as Ctrl+Alt (Windows reliably; Linux browsers usually report it as AltGraph alone). Because `[` matters when typing Markdown, and the WYSIWYG editor is contenteditable, which `shouldIgnoreShortcut` does not cover, the App.svelte dispatch entry also skips when `getModifierState("AltGraph")` is true and ⌘/Meta is not held (Firefox on macOS may flag Option as AltGraph, which would otherwise kill ⌘⌥F). Whether each OS and browser pair actually reports AltGraph there is unverified, so remapping is the fallback. The matcher itself stays pure and still matches AltGr+F; `useAppShortcuts.test.ts` pins that deliberately.
**Cross-references:** ADR-029 (Action Registry — superseded in part), ADR-037 (Layout Model — rune store over settings, the persistence pattern reused here).


## ADR-042: Markdown Fidelity — Raw-Construct Passthrough + Visibility Toggle

**Status:** Accepted (2026-06-03)

**Context:** Tandem wires `remark-gfm` into both the parser and stringifier, so every CommonMark + GFM construct becomes correct mdast. But a construct only round-trips if **both** the mdast↔Y.Doc mapping (`src/server/file-io/mdast-ydoc.ts`) and the Tiptap editor schema support it. Several constructs were parsed but then **silently dropped** on the way into the Y.Doc because their mdast nodes carry no `.value` string and the `default` cases only preserved value-bearing nodes: **footnote references + definitions**, **reference-style links + their definitions** (`linkReference`/`imageReference`/`definition`), and **inline HTML**. They vanished entirely on the first save. Nested inline images degraded to alt-text (URL/title lost). Issue #981 is the audit umbrella; task lists are carved out to #982.

**Decision:** Preserve every unsupported construct as **verbatim markdown source**, re-emitted as an mdast `html` node on save (the `mdast-util-to-markdown` html handler is literally `return node.value || ''` — it never escapes), and surface a show/hide toggle. Two carriers, mirroring the pre-existing `markdownHtml` raw-HTML-block mechanism:

- **Block-level raw** (`footnoteDefinition`, `definition`, unknown structured blocks) → stored as a `paragraph` carrying a new boolean **`markdownRaw`** attribute; serialized via `serializeMdastBlock` (wrap in `root`, `trimEnd`).
- **Inline raw** (`footnoteReference`, `linkReference`, `imageReference`, nested inline image, inline `html`) → stored as text under a new **`rawMarkdown`** mark (a standalone `Mark.create`, added to the server `ALL_MARKS` allowlist so `buildAttrs` emits it; the Tiptap mark name byte-matches the delta key); serialized via `serializeMdastInline` (wrap in `root > paragraph`, trim).

**Why the `html`-node wrapper (do not "simplify" it away):** gfm's own handlers would serialize an unwrapped structured node correctly too, but the wrapper earns its keep on the **forward** path — it stores the source as real Y.Doc *text* so flat offsets / `getElementText()` include it (the annotation coordinate system stays aligned) and the visibility toggle has a DOM anchor — and on the **reverse** path it bypasses the project's custom `text` escaper. Storing as text/marks (never `insertEmbed`) is the load-bearing coordinate-safety property: an embed would collapse the run to flat-length 1 and desync every later annotation.

**Visibility toggle:** a new `showRawMarkdown` setting (default on) flips a `hide-raw-md` class on the `.editor-scroll` wrapper; `editor.css` then `display:none`s `.tandem-raw-md` spans and `[data-markdown-raw]` paragraphs (CSS only — the source is always in the Y.Doc and saves regardless of the toggle). Surfaced in Appearance settings (`appearance-show-raw-markdown`).

**Documented normalizations (deliberate, not loss):** `remark-stringify` canonicalizes setext→ATX headings, indented→fenced code, bullet/emphasis markers to `-`/`*`, hard-break style, entity decoding, autolinks to angle form (`<https://…>`), and loose-list paragraphs → tight (blank lines between list items are dropped; `spread: false` is hardcoded in `yDocToMdast`). The fidelity test asserts **idempotency + content-preservation**, not byte-identity to hand-authored input.

**Amendment (2026-08-14, #1448): two claims in that paragraph were wrong, and the test contract in its last sentence is what let them stand.**

"Idempotency + content-preservation, not byte-identity" sounds like a modest, honest contract. It is not: **every defect found in #1448 satisfies it.** Each one mangles the document exactly once and is then a stable fixed point, so `pass2 === pass1` holds and the suite stays green while the file on disk is wrong. The contract could not fail, which is why nothing failed for months. It is replaced by **byte-identity on the first pass** for anything a reader would notice, measured by `tests/server/file-io/roundtrip-corpus.test.ts` and `tests/client/editor-roundtrip.test.ts`.

Three specific corrections:

- **"Hard-break style" was never a normalization.** What actually happened is that the *editor* converted **soft line wraps into hard breaks** — a semantic change, not a style choice. A soft wrap lives in the Y.Doc as a literal `\n` inside a `Y.XmlText`; a hard break is a sibling `Y.XmlElement("hardBreak")`. The Y.Doc distinguishes them; ProseMirror does not, so its DOM re-read split paragraph text on newlines. Saving then wrote a trailing `\` onto every wrapped line and renderers broke the paragraph at the author's wrap column. Fixed by declaring `whitespace: "pre"` on the paragraph node. Genuine hard-break *style* (`  ` vs `\`) is still normalized and that part stands.
- **The "no silent drop" guarantee did not hold for the raw carriers this ADR introduces.** `getElementPlainText` read only `Y.XmlText` children, so a newline held as a sibling `hardBreak` was dropped without warning and every multi-line raw block collapsed onto one line (#1458) — the exact failure mode this ADR exists to prevent, in the exact constructs it was written to protect.

- **Loose→tight was never a normalization either, and is now fixed.** `spread` was hardcoded `false` on both the list and each item in `yDocToMdast`, so every loose list in 56 of this repo's files was rewritten tight on the first save. It is now carried through the Y.Doc and declared on the Tiptap nodes (`ListSpreadExtension`), because an attribute the client schema does not declare is discarded by `computeAttrs` before the DOM is ever involved and then pruned from the Y.Doc — the same mechanism that destroyed table alignment.

**The replacement contract, in full.** A difference is either **visible** — a reader opening the file in another editor or viewer would see it — or **invisible**. Visible differences are defects and are held at zero by the corpus. Invisible ones are canonicalization: they are permitted, they must be idempotent (change once, then hold), and they are enumerated here so they are a stated behaviour rather than a discovery.

The invisible set, measured across all 245 tracked `.md` files in this repo:

- Marker and structure style with no mdast representation: setext→ATX headings, indented→fenced code, bullet/emphasis/fence/ordered markers, `***`→`---`, blank-line runs collapsed, entity decoding, autolinks to angle form.
- Table geometry: hand-authored `|---|---|` cannot be reproduced, and a row's trailing empty cell is made explicit. Cell *padding* no longer churns (`tablePipeAlign: false`), which was 112 of the 201 originally-differing files; alignment colons survive (they are carried as a declared `align` attribute).
- Code-span style: padding spaces inside a fence, a longer-than-minimal fence, and a span wrapped across a source line coming back on one line. CommonMark renders all three identically.
- Mark order where the source cannot be recovered: two marks covering exactly the same run come back in a fixed order (`~~**x**~~` → `**~~x~~**`). A delta segment's attributes are a set — Yjs does not record which mark opened first. Where run *lengths* differ, nesting IS recovered; that was the V5 defect and it is fixed.
- Escape noise: a literal backtick, a `\[label]` matching a real definition, and a `\@` before a host-shaped string all keep their backslash. Each is a deliberate over-keep — see the `text` handler in `markdown.ts`, whose rule 3 documents two corruptions caused by trying to be tidier.

Everything above renders identically. Recovering any of it needs a per-node source-marker layer, which is a materially larger project and is out of scope.

**Line endings are preserved, not normalized** (`src/server/file-io/line-endings.ts`): the dominant ending is detected at load and restored at save, while the model itself is always LF. A CRLF file previously came back mixed — block separators LF, intra-paragraph soft wraps CRLF — which is worse than either pure form and invisible to a repo corpus, since `.gitattributes` pins `*.md text eol=lf`. The detected set is `\n`, `\r\n` **and bare `\r`** (classic Mac). The third member is not hypothetical tidiness: `toLf` collapses `\r` like any other ending, so while the type admitted only two forms a lone-CR file had no name to be recorded under and was rewritten to LF on every line, silently and with no path back. A normalization the type system cannot express is a normalization nothing can undo.

**A persisted session is stamped with `DOCUMENT_MODEL_REVISION`.** `ydocState` is a bare `Y.encodeStateAsUpdate` of an already-parsed document, so none of the above reaches it; a session written under an older revision is discarded on reopen in favour of a fresh parse, unless it is `dirty` or an `upload://` path, where it is the only copy of the content.

**Deferred (#982):** GFM task lists / checkboxes need a first-class `TaskList`/`TaskItem` node and `checked` mapping; today they degrade to plain bullets. This is a documented gap pinned by `markdown-fidelity.test.ts` so it can never become a silent drop.

**Consequences:** the round-trip is a stable fixed point (re-open re-parses the emitted source into the same gfm nodes and re-stores them). Coverage: `tests/fixtures/markdown-fidelity.md` + `markdown-fidelity.test.ts` (every construct, idempotency, the #982 gap) and `markdown-raw-constructs.test.ts` (forward/reverse mapping + slice-level flat-offset stability). New settings fields must be enumerated in `normalizeKnownFields` (an allowlist) or they are silently dropped at runtime — `showRawMarkdown` is pinned by a presence/default regression.

**Cross-references:** ADR-027 (note privacy — raw passthrough is a view/serialization concern, not annotation data), ADR-031 (origin tagging — file-sync/internal writes), #981 (audit umbrella), #982 (task lists), #605 / lessons #69 (the remark-stringify escaper the wrapper bypasses).

## ADR-043: Updater — No Rollback, No In-Updater Post-Restart Health Probe (v1)

**Status:** Accepted (2026-06-07)

**Context:** The Tauri auto-updater path (`perform_install` in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`) runs `kill_sidecar` → `wait_for_port_release` (+ Windows `wait_for_sidecar_unlock`; all three moved to `src-tauri/src/sidecar.rs` in Unit 11e) → `update.download_and_install` (minisign-verified) → `app.restart()`. Issue #925 flagged two gaps: (1) no rollback if `app.restart()` fails to relaunch a broken/corrupt new binary, and (2) no health-poll *inside* the updater path after restart.

**Decision:** Ship v1 with current behavior unchanged (option **(c)** of #925). The decisive constraint is that **Tauri v2 `AppHandle::restart()` is divergent** — it exits and relaunches the process without returning (tauri-apps/tauri #12310/#13923/#11392). Therefore: (2) an in-`perform_install` post-restart probe is unreachable dead code by construction; and (1) rollback cannot be driven from the old process, which is gone the instant `restart()` runs — true rollback would require a standalone watchdog/bootstrapper process plus a `.previous` binary copy and platform-specific swap-back logic. Neither the [Tauri v2 updater docs](https://v2.tauri.app/plugin/updater/) nor [CrabNebula's auto-updates guide](https://docs.crabnebula.dev/guides/auto-updates-tauri) recommend either; both stop at `download_and_install()` + relaunch with no rollback or post-restart verification.

**Why this is acceptable:** The post-restart health verification #925 asks for **already exists for the sidecar** — the relaunched process's `start_sidecar` → `wait_for_health` (bounded) and the `MAX_RESTARTS` retry loop, which surfaces a native "Server Error" dialog on exhausted failure. (The `sidecar-restart-failed` WebView event is emitted only from the manual `restart_sidecar` command, **not** this startup path.) The only uncovered case is the Tauri *shell* failing to relaunch at all, which is rare (reached only after signature verification; a binary that won't run on the target OS fails the *first* launch, not a relaunch) and unobservable from Rust without an external supervisor.

**Deferred follow-up (optional, not v1-blocking — filed as #1118, 2026-06-11):** The one in-process-only hardening that survives the divergent-`restart()` constraint is a persisted "pending update" marker — write a sentinel before `restart()`; the next boot clears it on version-match or surfaces a one-time "your update may not have completed — report a bug" banner on mismatch. Diagnostic/recovery-hint only (no binary swap), sketched in `docs/spikes/updater-rollback-healthpoll-audit.md` §6, and must be compiled + runtime-verified in a real Tauri build before landing.
